It does make me run late sometimes, but I just feel like I have to do it to get rid of that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do these doubts feel intrusive and unwanted, even though you recognize they may not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I was saying before about the medicine and my classroom setup... I really don't want to be thinking about it. The thoughts just kind of force their way in. And logically, I know it doesn't make a lot of sense, because I know I probably did everything right the first time. But no matter what I tell myself, that doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these doubts and checking or mental reviewing take up on a typical day?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually timed it. Um... it's not like it takes up half my day or anything. It's mostly just a few minutes here and there. Like, maybe an extra five or ten minutes before I leave the house in the morning, and a little bit of time at the end of the school day when I'm packing up my classroom. I haven't really added it all up to see the total time.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from these intrusive doubts and checking urges, do you regularly worry excessively about many different everyday areas of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. And logically, I know it doesn't make a lot of sense, because I know I probably did everything right the first time. But no matter what I tell myself, that doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these doubts and checking or mental reviewing take up on a typical day?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually timed it. Um... it's not like it takes up half my day or anything. It's mostly just a few minutes here and there. Like, maybe an extra five or ten minutes before I leave the house in the morning, and a little bit of time at the end of the school day when I'm packing up my classroom. I haven't really added it all up to see the total time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these intrusive doubts and checking urges, do you regularly worry excessively about many different everyday areas of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone else, I guess. Bills, or if a student is having a hard time. But it's not excessive. The main thing that really gets to me and makes me anxious is just the checking stuff I already mentioned. I don't spend a lot of time worrying about other things besides that.\n\nDoctor: How much do these thoughts and checking behaviors interfere with your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: It's frustrating, but I'm still getting everything done. I can still handle work and my family stuff. It's just... my mornings definitely take longer now. Because of the checking, I sometimes get to school feeling really rushed, like I'm already behind before the bell even rings. And it kind of bleeds into other things, too. It's just this annoying extra layer of stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience both the intrusive doubts and the repeated checking or mental reviewing, with the checking done to reduce the distress from those doubts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That's exactly it. When I get those doubts before I leave the house or my classroom, I'll go back over the same things a few times. I'll check my pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head. I basically keep doing it until I feel calm enough to move on. Doing all that checking is what relieves the stress, even though it's exactly what makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been occurring on most days for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't been keeping track of the exact days or... or how long it's been going on like this. I just know it's been happening recently, but I couldn't say for sure if it's been most days for the last two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But no matter what I tell myself, that doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: About how much time do these doubts and checking or mental reviewing take up on a typical day?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually timed it. Um... it's not like it takes up half my day or anything. It's mostly just a few minutes here and there. Like, maybe an extra five or ten minutes before I leave the house in the morning, and a little bit of time at the end of the school day when I'm packing up my classroom. I haven't really added it all up to see the total time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these intrusive doubts and checking urges, do you regularly worry excessively about many different everyday areas of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone else, I guess. Bills, or if a student is having a hard time. But it's not excessive. The main thing that really gets to me and makes me anxious is just the checking stuff I already mentioned. I don't spend a lot of time worrying about other things besides that.\n\nDoctor: How much do these thoughts and checking behaviors interfere with your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: It's frustrating, but I'm still getting everything done. I can still handle work and my family stuff. It's just... my mornings definitely take longer now. Because of the checking, I sometimes get to school feeling really rushed, like I'm already behind before the bell even rings. And it kind of bleeds into other things, too. Like when I'm prepping lessons or trying to do my journaling in the evening to unwind, it just doesn't feel as smooth or relaxing as it used to. It's just this annoying extra layer of stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience both the intrusive doubts and the repeated checking or mental reviewing, with the checking done to reduce the distress from those doubts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That's exactly it. When I get those doubts before I leave the house or my classroom, I'll go back over the same things a few times. I'll check my pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head. I basically keep doing it until I feel calm enough to move on. Doing all that checking is what relieves the stress, even though it's exactly what makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been occurring on most days for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't been keeping track of the exact days or... or how long it's been going on like this. I just know it's been happening recently, but I couldn't say for sure if it's been most days for the last two weeks.\n\nDoctor: When did these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors first begin?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure exactly when it started. It feels like it's just kind of crept up on me recently. I can't point to a specific day or week when it began.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, do the intrusive doubts and checking or mental reviewing take up at least an hour in total?\n\nPatient: I haven't really timed it, to be honest. It usually happens in little bursts, like right before I leave for work or between classes. I'm really not sure if all those moments add up to a full hour over the course of the whole day... I just haven't paid that close attention to the clock to know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been present on most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think you actually just asked me that a moment ago... and, yeah, I really still don't know for sure. Like I mentioned, I haven't been tracking it on a calendar or anything. It's definitely been happening lately, but I just can't say for certain if it's been most days for the past two weeks. I just haven't paid quite that much attention to the exact timeline.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven't really added it all up to see the total time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these intrusive doubts and checking urges, do you regularly worry excessively about many different everyday areas of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone else, I guess. Bills, or if a student is having a hard time. But it's not excessive. The main thing that really gets to me and makes me anxious is just the checking stuff I already mentioned. I don't spend a lot of time worrying about other things besides that.\n\nDoctor: How much do these thoughts and checking behaviors interfere with your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: It's frustrating, but I'm still getting everything done. I can still handle work and my family stuff. It's just... my mornings definitely take longer now. It's just this annoying extra layer of stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience both the intrusive doubts and the repeated checking or mental reviewing, with the checking done to reduce the distress from those doubts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I'll check my pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head. I basically keep doing it until I feel calm enough to move on. Doing all that checking is what relieves the stress, even though it's exactly what makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been occurring on most days for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't been keeping track of the exact days or... or how long it's been going on like this. I just haven't paid that close attention to the clock to know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been present on most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think you actually just asked me that a moment ago... and, yeah, I really still don't know for sure. It's just this annoying extra layer of stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience both the intrusive doubts and the repeated checking or mental reviewing, with the checking done to reduce the distress from those doubts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That's exactly it. When I get those doubts before I leave the house or my classroom, I'll go back over the same things a few times. I'll check my pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head. I basically keep doing it until I feel calm enough to move on. Doing all that checking is what relieves the stress, even though it's exactly what makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been occurring on most days for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't been keeping track of the exact days or... or how long it's been going on like this. I just know it's been happening recently, but I couldn't say for sure if it's been most days for the last two weeks.\n\nDoctor: When did these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors first begin?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure exactly when it started. It feels like it's just kind of crept up on me recently. I can't point to a specific day or week when it began.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, do the intrusive doubts and checking or mental reviewing take up at least an hour in total?\n\nPatient: I haven't really timed it, to be honest. It usually happens in little bursts, like right before I leave for work or between classes. I'm really not sure if all those moments add up to a full hour over the course of the whole day... I just haven't paid that close attention to the clock to know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been present on most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think you actually just asked me that a moment ago... and, yeah, I really still don't know for sure. I just haven't paid quite that much attention to the exact timeline.\n\nDoctor: In the past 14 days, on how many days did you experience the intrusive doubts and checking or mental reviewing?\n\nPatient: I... It's been happening, but I don't know exactly how many days out of the last fourteen.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\":\"Determine whether the obsessive thoughts and compulsive checking have persisted long enough to meet the duration requirement for a specific OCD diagnosis.\",\"question\":\"Have these intrusive doubts and checking behaviors been occurring for at least two weeks?\"\"}\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to get a timeline, but I really just don't have a clear answer for you. Um... well, we had a big staff meeting at school about three weeks ago. I remember that day clearly. But honestly, even thinking back to that... Yes, it's definitely in response to the doubts. It's not just a routine or something I prefer to do. Like, before I leave the house or my classroom, I get this feeling that I missed something important.
